Like... my heart follow-up appointments, or remembering to call the clinic back. Even when I'm just doing a repair job around the house, I'll show up to fix it and realize I didn't bring the right tools with me. It's just happening a bit too much lately, and I need to figure out what's going on.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about these memory lapses? For example, do you also forget things like where you put your keys, or whether you turned off the stove, or is it mostly things like appointments and tasks you need to remember?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really noticed it with things like my keys or the stove. I don't think I have a problem with that. It's mostly just... what I mentioned before. The appointments, or getting ready to do a project and forgetting what I need for it. I'm not really losing everyday objects around the house, as far as I know. It's just those bigger tasks and schedules that seem to slip my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things you’ve learned recently, or do you find yourself struggling to recall events from the recent past, like what you had for dinner yesterday or what happened earlier today?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I can remember what I had for dinner just fine, and I know exactly what I did this morning. It's not like I'm losing track of time or forgetting conversations I just had. My memory for that kind of everyday stuff seems perfectly normal to me. It really is just... well, those appointments and planning out what I need for my projects, like I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember what I asked you about your memory just before this question?\n\nPatient: Yeah, you just asked if I had trouble remembering things I learned recently, or if I forget recent events, like what I had for dinner yesterday or what I did earlier today. I remember you asking that just fine. Like I said, my memory for conversations and everyday things like that doesn't seem to be the issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things you had planned to do, like taking medication at a certain time or sending an email you meant to send later?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly the kind of stuff I'm talking about. It's why I'm here, honestly. I keep forgetting things I really shouldn't be forgetting. Like, I'll completely miss my follow-up appointments for my heart, or I'll mean to call the clinic back and it just... never happens. Or I'll get ready to do a repair job around the house, and I show up without the right tools because I just didn't think to grab them. It's those kinds of planned things that just fall right out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to start tasks that require planning, like organizing a project or following a multi-step process, or do you often start them but not finish?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have a hard time starting things. I usually just jump right in and get to work. And I finish what I start, too. It's just... well, like I mentioned, I might show up to do a job and realize I forgot to bring the right tools. But the actual starting and finishing part isn't the problem. I don't sit around putting it off or leaving things half-done.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you intended to do later, even if you wrote them down or set a reminder for yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. If I actually take a minute to write it down—like putting a note on the fridge or something—I usually remember to do it. \n\nThe issue is more that I just... I don't always think to write it down in the first place. But if I do have a reminder right there staring at me, I'm pretty good about getting it done. I don't usually forget it then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things you intended to do later, like taking medication at a specific time or attending appointments, even when you didn't rely on a reminder?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying a minute ago... if I don't have a reminder, that's exactly when I forget. If I don't write it down, I'll completely miss an appointment or forget to make a phone call. So, yes, if I'm just relying on my own memory without a note or something, it usually just falls right out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to remember things you need to do later when you're busy or distracted, or does it happen even when you're not doing anything else?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if being busy makes a difference, to be honest. I haven't really paid much attention to that. \n\nI think it just happens either way. It doesn't really make a difference if I'm distracted or just sitting around doing nothing. If I need to call someone or pick something up at the store, and I didn't make a note of it, I'll probably forget it. Being busy doesn't really seem to be the cause of it, it just happens either way.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember events from your past, like what you did last week or important moments from years ago?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, my memory for the past is fine. It's all still up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you forget things you intended to do when you move from one place to another, like leaving a room and forgetting why you went in there?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say that happens to me much. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Well, what brings me in is that I just keep forgetting things I really shouldn’t. Important stuff, you know? Like... my heart follow-up appointments, or remembering to call the clinic back. Even when I'm just doing a repair job around the house, I'll show up to fix it and realize I didn't bring the right tools with me. It's just happening a bit too much lately, and I need to figure out what's going on.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about these memory lapses? For example, do you also forget things like where you put your keys, or whether you turned off the stove, or is it mostly things like appointments and tasks you need to remember?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really noticed it with things like my keys or the stove. I don't think I have a problem with that. It's mostly just... what I mentioned before. The appointments, or getting ready to do a project and forgetting what I need for it. I'm not really losing everyday objects around the house, as far as I know. It's just those bigger tasks and schedules that seem to slip my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things you’ve learned recently, or do you find yourself struggling to recall events from the recent past, like what you had for dinner yesterday or what happened earlier today?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I can remember what I had for dinner just fine, and I know exactly what I did this morning. It's not like I'm losing track of time or forgetting conversations I just had. My memory for that kind of everyday stuff seems perfectly normal to me. It really is just... well, those appointments and planning out what I need for my projects, like I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember what I asked you about your memory just before this question?\n\nPatient: Yeah, you just asked if I had trouble remembering things I learned recently, or if I forget recent events, like what I had for dinner yesterday or what I did earlier today. I remember you asking that just fine. Like I said, my memory for conversations and everyday things like that doesn't seem to be the issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things you had planned to do, like taking medication at a certain time or sending an email you meant to send later?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly the kind of stuff I'm talking about. It's why I'm here, honestly. I keep forgetting things I really shouldn't be forgetting. Like, I'll completely miss my follow-up appointments for my heart, or I'll mean to call the clinic back and it just... never happens. Or I'll get ready to do a repair job around the house, and I show up without the right tools because I just didn't think to grab them. It's those kinds of planned things that just fall right out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to start tasks that require planning, like organizing a project or following a multi-step process, or do you often start them but not finish?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have a hard time starting things. I usually just jump right in and get to work. And I finish what I start, too. It's just... well, like I mentioned, I might show up to do a job and realize I forgot to bring the right tools. But the actual starting and finishing part isn't the problem. I don't sit around putting it off or leaving things half-done.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you intended to do later, even if you wrote them down or set a reminder for yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. If I actually take a minute to write it down—like putting a note on the fridge or something—I usually remember to do it. \n\nThe issue is more that I just... I don't always think to write it down in the first place. But if I do have a reminder right there staring at me, I'm pretty good about getting it done. I don't usually forget it then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things you intended to do later, like taking medication at a specific time or attending appointments, even when you didn't rely on a reminder?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying a minute ago... if I don't have a reminder, that's exactly when I forget. If I don't write it down, I'll completely miss an appointment or forget to make a phone call. So, yes, if I'm just relying on my own memory without a note or something, it usually just falls right out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to remember things you need to do later when you're busy or distracted, or does it happen even when you're not doing anything else?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if being busy makes a difference, to be honest. I haven't really paid much attention to that. \n\nI think it just happens either way. I could be out in the garage working on something, or just sitting in my recliner not doing much of anything... if I didn't write it down, it's just gone. So, I guess it doesn't really matter what I'm doing at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you need to do in the future, like making a call or buying something, even when you're not distracted or busy with something else?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, that's pretty much what I was just saying. It doesn't really make a difference if I'm distracted or just sitting around doing nothing. If I need to call someone or pick something up at the store, and I didn't make a note of it, I'll probably forget it. Being busy doesn't really seem to be the cause of it, it just happens either way.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember events from your past, like what you did last week or important moments from years ago?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, my memory for the past is fine. I can tell you what I did last week or, you know, things from years ago without much trouble at all. \n\nIt's really just the things I haven't done yet that give me grief. But looking back... no, I don't have any problems remembering all that. It's all still up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you forget things you intended to do when you move from one place to another, like leaving a room and forgetting why you went in there?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say that happens to me much. I mean... maybe once in a blue moon, like anybody else, but it's not a real problem for me. \n\nUsually, if I get up out of my chair to go get a tool from the garage or something from the kitchen, I know what I'm after. Like I was saying earlier, my trouble is more about just forgetting to do things later on if I don't write them down. But walking into a room and losing my train of thought just from walking through the door? No, I don't really deal with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to form clear intentions to do things later on, like making a mental note to call someone when you get home or reminding yourself to pick something up on the way back?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that's the issue. I mean, I can make the mental note just fine. I'll tell myself, 'Make sure you grab milk on the way back,' or whatever it is. But forming the thought to do it in the first place? No, I don't have any trouble with that part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, compared to remembering to do something when a certain event happens, like calling someone when you get home?\n\nPatient: I can't say I've really noticed a difference between the two, to be honest. Um... whether it's looking at the clock to do something at a specific time or waiting until I get home to do it, it all kind of plays out the same for me. \n\nLike I was saying before, if I don't have it written down on a piece of paper somewhere, it's probably going to slip my mind either way. So, no, I wouldn't say one of those is harder than the other.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Being busy doesn't really seem to be the cause of it, it just happens either way.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember events from your past, like what you did last week or important moments from years ago?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, my memory for the past is fine. I can tell you what I did last week or, you know, things from years ago without much trouble at all. \n\nIt's really just the things I haven't done yet that give me grief. But looking back... no, I don't have any problems remembering all that. It's all still up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you forget things you intended to do when you move from one place to another, like leaving a room and forgetting why you went in there?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say that happens to me much. No, I don't really deal with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to form clear intentions to do things later on, like making a mental note to call someone when you get home or reminding yourself to pick something up on the way back?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that's the issue. The intention is definitely there. \n\nIt's just... well, like we were talking about, if I don't actually put it on a piece of paper, it just disappears from my head by the time I actually get home. But forming the thought to do it in the first place? No, I don't have any trouble with that part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, compared to remembering to do something when a certain event happens, like calling someone when you get home?\n\nPatient: I can't say I've really noticed a difference between the two, to be honest. Um... whether it's looking at the clock to do something at a specific time or waiting until I get home to do it, it all kind of plays out the same for me. \n\nLike I was saying before, if I don't have it written down on a piece of paper somewhere, it's probably going to slip my mind either way. I can remember what I had for dinner just fine, and I know exactly what I did this morning. It's not like I'm losing track of time or forgetting conversations I just had. My memory for that kind of everyday stuff seems perfectly normal to me. It really is just... well, those appointments and planning out what I need for my projects, like I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember what I asked you about your memory just before this question?\n\nPatient: Yeah, you just asked if I had trouble remembering things I learned recently, or if I forget recent events, like what I had for dinner yesterday or what I did earlier today. I remember you asking that just fine. Like I said, my memory for conversations and everyday things like that doesn't seem to be the issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things you had planned to do, like taking medication at a certain time or sending an email you meant to send later?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly the kind of stuff I'm talking about. It's why I'm here, honestly. I keep forgetting things I really shouldn't be forgetting. Like, I'll completely miss my follow-up appointments for my heart, or I'll mean to call the clinic back and it just... never happens. Or I'll get ready to do a repair job around the house, and I show up without the right tools because I just didn't think to grab them. It's those kinds of planned things that just fall right out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to start tasks that require planning, like organizing a project or following a multi-step process, or do you often start them but not finish?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have a hard time starting things. I usually just jump right in and get to work. And I finish what I start, too. It's just... well, like I mentioned, I might show up to do a job and realize I forgot to bring the right tools. But the actual starting and finishing part isn't the problem. I don't sit around putting it off or leaving things half-done.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you intended to do later, even if you wrote them down or set a reminder for yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. If I actually take a minute to write it down—like putting a note on the fridge or something—I usually remember to do it. \n\nThe issue is more that I just... I don't always think to write it down in the first place. But if I do have a reminder right there staring at me, I'm pretty good about getting it done. I don't usually forget it then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things you intended to do later, like taking medication at a specific time or attending appointments, even when you didn't rely on a reminder?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying a minute ago... if I don't have a reminder, that's exactly when I forget. If I don't write it down, I'll completely miss an appointment or forget to make a phone call. So, yes, if I'm just relying on my own memory without a note or something, it usually just falls right out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to remember things you need to do later when you're busy or distracted, or does it happen even when you're not doing anything else?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if being busy makes a difference, to be honest. I haven't really paid much attention to that. \n\nI think it just happens either way. I could be out in the garage working on something, or just sitting in my recliner not doing much of anything... if I didn't write it down, it's just gone. So, I guess it doesn't really matter what I'm doing at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you need to do in the future, like making a call or buying something, even when you're not distracted or busy with something else?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, that's pretty much what I was just saying. It doesn't really make a difference if I'm distracted or just sitting around doing nothing. If I need to call someone or pick something up at the store, and I didn't make a note of it, I'll probably forget it. Being busy doesn't really seem to be the cause of it, it just happens either way.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember events from your past, like what you did last week or important moments from years ago?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, my memory for the past is fine. I can tell you what I did last week or, you know, things from years ago without much trouble at all. \n\nIt's really just the things I haven't done yet that give me grief. But looking back... no, I don't have any problems remembering all that. It's all still up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you forget things you intended to do when you move from one place to another, like leaving a room and forgetting why you went in there?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say that happens to me much. I mean... maybe once in a blue moon, like anybody else, but it's not a real problem for me. \n\nUsually, if I get up out of my chair to go get a tool from the garage or something from the kitchen, I know what I'm after. Like I was saying earlier, my trouble is more about just forgetting to do things later on if I don't write them down. But walking into a room and losing my train of thought just from walking through the door? No, I don't really deal with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to form clear intentions to do things later on, like making a mental note to call someone when you get home or reminding yourself to pick something up on the way back?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that's the issue. No, I don't have any trouble with that part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, compared to remembering to do something when a certain event happens, like calling someone when you get home?\n\nPatient: I can't say I've really noticed a difference between the two, to be honest. So, no, I wouldn't say one of those is harder than the other.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it especially hard to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, even when you really try to remember without writing it down?\n\nPatient: Well, I think that goes right back to what I was just saying a second ago. It really is just... well, those appointments and planning out what I need for my projects, like I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember what I asked you about your memory just before this question?\n\nPatient: Yeah, you just asked if I had trouble remembering things I learned recently, or if I forget recent events, like what I had for dinner yesterday or what I did earlier today. I remember you asking that just fine. Like I said, my memory for conversations and everyday things like that doesn't seem to be the issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things you had planned to do, like taking medication at a certain time or sending an email you meant to send later?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly the kind of stuff I'm talking about. It's why I'm here, honestly. I keep forgetting things I really shouldn't be forgetting. Like, I'll completely miss my follow-up appointments for my heart, or I'll mean to call the clinic back and it just... never happens. Or I'll get ready to do a repair job around the house, and I show up without the right tools because I just didn't think to grab them. It's those kinds of planned things that just fall right out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to start tasks that require planning, like organizing a project or following a multi-step process, or do you often start them but not finish?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have a hard time starting things. I usually just jump right in and get to work. And I finish what I start, too. It's just... well, like I mentioned, I might show up to do a job and realize I forgot to bring the right tools. But the actual starting and finishing part isn't the problem. I don't sit around putting it off or leaving things half-done.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you intended to do later, even if you wrote them down or set a reminder for yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. If I actually take a minute to write it down—like putting a note on the fridge or something—I usually remember to do it. \n\nThe issue is more that I just... I don't always think to write it down in the first place. But if I do have a reminder right there staring at me, I'm pretty good about getting it done. I don't usually forget it then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things you intended to do later, like taking medication at a specific time or attending appointments, even when you didn't rely on a reminder?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying a minute ago... if I don't have a reminder, that's exactly when I forget. If I don't write it down, I'll completely miss an appointment or forget to make a phone call. So, yes, if I'm just relying on my own memory without a note or something, it usually just falls right out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to remember things you need to do later when you're busy or distracted, or does it happen even when you're not doing anything else?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if being busy makes a difference, to be honest. I haven't really paid much attention to that. \n\nI think it just happens either way. I could be out in the garage working on something, or just sitting in my recliner not doing much of anything... if I didn't write it down, it's just gone. So, I guess it doesn't really matter what I'm doing at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you need to do in the future, like making a call or buying something, even when you're not distracted or busy with something else?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, that's pretty much what I was just saying. It doesn't really make a difference if I'm distracted or just sitting around doing nothing. If I need to call someone or pick something up at the store, and I didn't make a note of it, I'll probably forget it. Being busy doesn't really seem to be the cause of it, it just happens either way.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember events from your past, like what you did last week or important moments from years ago?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, my memory for the past is fine. I can tell you what I did last week or, you know, things from years ago without much trouble at all. \n\nIt's really just the things I haven't done yet that give me grief. But looking back... no, I don't have any problems remembering all that. It's all still up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you forget things you intended to do when you move from one place to another, like leaving a room and forgetting why you went in there?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say that happens to me much. I mean... maybe once in a blue moon, like anybody else, but it's not a real problem for me. \n\nUsually, if I get up out of my chair to go get a tool from the garage or something from the kitchen, I know what I'm after. Like I was saying earlier, my trouble is more about just forgetting to do things later on if I don't write them down. But walking into a room and losing my train of thought just from walking through the door? No, I don't really deal with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to form clear intentions to do things later on, like making a mental note to call someone when you get home or reminding yourself to pick something up on the way back?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that's the issue. I mean, I can make the mental note just fine. I'll tell myself, 'Make sure you grab milk on the way back,' or whatever it is. The intention is definitely there. \n\nIt's just... well, like we were talking about, if I don't actually put it on a piece of paper, it just disappears from my head by the time I actually get home. But forming the thought to do it in the first place? No, I don't have any trouble with that part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, compared to remembering to do something when a certain event happens, like calling someone when you get home?\n\nPatient: I can't say I've really noticed a difference between the two, to be honest. Um... whether it's looking at the clock to do something at a specific time or waiting until I get home to do it, it all kind of plays out the same for me. \n\nLike I was saying before, if I don't have it written down on a piece of paper somewhere, it's probably going to slip my mind either way. So, no, I wouldn't say one of those is harder than the other.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it especially hard to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, even when you really try to remember without writing it down?\n\nPatient: Well, I think that goes right back to what I was just saying a second ago. It really just comes down to whether I wrote it down or not. \n\nI wouldn't say it's especially harder just because it's a specific time on the clock. I mean... if I try to just rely on my memory and tell myself, 'Don't forget that pill at 10 AM,' yeah, I'll probably end up missing it. But that's true for pretty much anything I try to keep in my head without making a note of it. It's not the specific time that makes it worse, it's just the trying to do it without my piece of paper.\n\nDoctor: If you're interrupted while making plans or thinking about something you need to do later, do you find that you forget what you were planning to do once the interruption is over?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't really noticed that happening. If the phone rings or someone stops to talk to me while I'm trying to figure out my plans, I can usually go right back to what I was thinking about once the interruption is over. \n\nIt doesn't just wipe it from my mind right then and there. Like I was saying, the real problem is what happens hours later if I didn't manage to get it down on paper. But just losing my train of thought because of a quick interruption? No, I wouldn't say I have a problem with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it especially hard to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, even when you really try to remember without writing it down?\n\nPatient: I think you might have just asked me that one, Doc. But, um, yeah, my answer is pretty much the same. \n\nIt's not the specific time on the clock that makes it hard. If I try to remember to do *anything* without writing it down, I'm probably going to forget it. So, no, taking a pill at 10 AM isn't any harder than anything else I try to just keep in my head. It really just all comes down to whether I made a note on my piece of paper or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever forgotten something so important that it put you or someone else in danger, like turning off the stove or taking the right medication at the right dose?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I have. I mean, I might forget to pick up milk or call someone back if I don't write it down, but the big things... no. I've never left the stove on or messed up my medication or anything dangerous like that. I guess I'm pretty careful about the stuff that really matters.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, I wouldn't say I have a problem with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it especially hard to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, even when you really try to remember without writing it down?\n\nPatient: I think you might have just asked me that one, Doc. But, um, yeah, my answer is pretty much the same. \n\nIt's not the specific time on the clock that makes it hard. If I try to remember to do *anything* without writing it down, I'm probably going to forget it. So, no, taking a pill at 10 AM isn't any harder than anything else I try to just keep in my head. It really just all comes down to whether I made a note on my piece of paper or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever forgotten something so important that it put you or someone else in danger, like turning off the stove or taking the right medication at the right dose?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I have. I mean, I might forget to pick up milk or call someone back if I don't write it down, but the big things... no. I've never left the stove on or messed up my medication or anything dangerous like that. I guess I'm pretty careful about the stuff that really matters.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say you forget things you need to do in the future even when you try to remember them without any external help, like notes or reminders?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 68 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I feel like we're kind of going in circles here, Doc. Like I've been saying, if I don't write it down on my piece of paper, I'm going to forget it. It doesn't matter how hard I try to just keep it in my head. If there's no note, it's gone. So, yes, if I don't use my notes, I forget what I need to do.

If I need to call someone or pick something up at the store, and I didn't make a note of it, I'll probably forget it. I can tell you what I did last week or, you know, things from years ago without much trouble at all. \n\nIt's really just the things I haven't done yet that give me grief. It's all still up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you forget things you intended to do when you move from one place to another, like leaving a room and forgetting why you went in there?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say that happens to me much. I mean... maybe once in a blue moon, like anybody else, but it's not a real problem for me. \n\nUsually, if I get up out of my chair to go get a tool from the garage or something from the kitchen, I know what I'm after. Like I was saying earlier, my trouble is more about just forgetting to do things later on if I don't write them down. But walking into a room and losing my train of thought just from walking through the door? No, I don't really deal with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to form clear intentions to do things later on, like making a mental note to call someone when you get home or reminding yourself to pick something up on the way back?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that's the issue. I mean, I can make the mental note just fine. I'll tell myself, 'Make sure you grab milk on the way back,' or whatever it is. The intention is definitely there. \n\nIt's just... well, like we were talking about, if I don't actually put it on a piece of paper, it just disappears from my head by the time I actually get home. But forming the thought to do it in the first place? No, I don't have any trouble with that part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, compared to remembering to do something when a certain event happens, like calling someone when you get home?\n\nPatient: I can't say I've really noticed a difference between the two, to be honest. Um... whether it's looking at the clock to do something at a specific time or waiting until I get home to do it, it all kind of plays out the same for me. \n\nLike I was saying before, if I don't have it written down on a piece of paper somewhere, it's probably going to slip my mind either way. So, no, I wouldn't say one of those is harder than the other.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it especially hard to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, even when you really try to remember without writing it down?\n\nPatient: Well, I think that goes right back to what I was just saying a second ago. It really just comes down to whether I wrote it down or not. \n\nI wouldn't say it's especially harder just because it's a specific time on the clock. I mean... if I try to just rely on my memory and tell myself, 'Don't forget that pill at 10 AM,' yeah, I'll probably end up missing it. But that's true for pretty much anything I try to keep in my head without making a note of it. It's not the specific time that makes it worse, it's just the trying to do it without my piece of paper.\n\nDoctor: If you're interrupted while making plans or thinking about something you need to do later, do you find that you forget what you were planning to do once the interruption is over?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't really noticed that happening. I don't sit around putting it off or leaving things half-done.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you intended to do later, even if you wrote them down or set a reminder for yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. If I actually take a minute to write it down—like putting a note on the fridge or something—I usually remember to do it. \n\nThe issue is more that I just... I don't always think to write it down in the first place. But if I do have a reminder right there staring at me, I'm pretty good about getting it done. I don't usually forget it then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things you intended to do later, like taking medication at a specific time or attending appointments, even when you didn't rely on a reminder?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying a minute ago... if I don't have a reminder, that's exactly when I forget. If I don't write it down, I'll completely miss an appointment or forget to make a phone call. So, yes, if I'm just relying on my own memory without a note or something, it usually just falls right out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to remember things you need to do later when you're busy or distracted, or does it happen even when you're not doing anything else?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if being busy makes a difference, to be honest. I haven't really paid much attention to that. \n\nI think it just happens either way. I could be out in the garage working on something, or just sitting in my recliner not doing much of anything... if I didn't write it down, it's just gone. So, I guess it doesn't really matter what I'm doing at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you need to do in the future, like making a call or buying something, even when you're not distracted or busy with something else?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, that's pretty much what I was just saying. It doesn't really make a difference if I'm distracted or just sitting around doing nothing. If I need to call someone or pick something up at the store, and I didn't make a note of it, I'll probably forget it. Being busy doesn't really seem to be the cause of it, it just happens either way.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember events from your past, like what you did last week or important moments from years ago?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, my memory for the past is fine. I can tell you what I did last week or, you know, things from years ago without much trouble at all. \n\nIt's really just the things I haven't done yet that give me grief. But looking back... no, I don't have any problems remembering all that. It's all still up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you forget things you intended to do when you move from one place to another, like leaving a room and forgetting why you went in there?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say that happens to me much. I mean... maybe once in a blue moon, like anybody else, but it's not a real problem for me. \n\nUsually, if I get up out of my chair to go get a tool from the garage or something from the kitchen, I know what I'm after. Like I was saying earlier, my trouble is more about just forgetting to do things later on if I don't write them down. But walking into a room and losing my train of thought just from walking through the door? No, I don't really deal with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to form clear intentions to do things later on, like making a mental note to call someone when you get home or reminding yourself to pick something up on the way back?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that's the issue. I mean, I can make the mental note just fine. I'll tell myself, 'Make sure you grab milk on the way back,' or whatever it is. The intention is definitely there. \n\nIt's just... well, like we were talking about, if I don't actually put it on a piece of paper, it just disappears from my head by the time I actually get home. But forming the thought to do it in the first place? No, I don't have any trouble with that part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, compared to remembering to do something when a certain event happens, like calling someone when you get home?\n\nPatient: I can't say I've really noticed a difference between the two, to be honest. Um... whether it's looking at the clock to do something at a specific time or waiting until I get home to do it, it all kind of plays out the same for me. \n\nLike I was saying before, if I don't have it written down on a piece of paper somewhere, it's probably going to slip my mind either way. So, no, I wouldn't say one of those is harder than the other.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it especially hard to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, even when you really try to remember without writing it down?\n\nPatient: Well, I think that goes right back to what I was just saying a second ago. It really just comes down to whether I wrote it down or not. \n\nI wouldn't say it's especially harder just because it's a specific time on the clock. I mean... if I try to just rely on my memory and tell myself, 'Don't forget that pill at 10 AM,' yeah, I'll probably end up missing it. But that's true for pretty much anything I try to keep in my head without making a note of it. It's not the specific time that makes it worse, it's just the trying to do it without my piece of paper.\n\nDoctor: If you're interrupted while making plans or thinking about something you need to do later, do you find that you forget what you were planning to do once the interruption is over?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't really noticed that happening. If the phone rings or someone stops to talk to me while I'm trying to figure out my plans, I can usually go right back to what I was thinking about once the interruption is over. \n\nIt doesn't just wipe it from my mind right then and there. Like I was saying, the real problem is what happens hours later if I didn't manage to get it down on paper. But just losing my train of thought because of a quick interruption? No, I wouldn't say I have a problem with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it especially hard to remember to do things at a specific time, like taking a pill at 10 AM, even when you really try to remember without writing it down?\n\nPatient: I think you might have just asked me that one, Doc. But, um, yeah, my answer is pretty much the same. \n\nIt's not the specific time on the clock that makes it hard. If I try to remember to do *anything* without writing it down, I'm probably going to forget it. So, no, taking a pill at 10 AM isn't any harder than anything else I try to just keep in my head. It really just all comes down to whether I made a note on my piece of paper or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever forgotten something so important that it put you or someone else in danger, like turning off the stove or taking the right medication at the right dose?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I have. I mean, I might forget to pick up milk or call someone back if I don't write it down, but the big things... no. I've never left the stove on or messed up my medication or anything dangerous like that. I guess I'm pretty careful about the stuff that really matters.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you forget things you need to do in the future even when you try to remember them without any external help, like notes or reminders?\n\nPatient: Well, I feel like we're kind of going in circles here, Doc. Like I've been saying, if I don't write it down on my piece of paper, I'm going to forget it. It doesn't matter how hard I try to just keep it in my head. If there's no note, it's gone. So, yes, if I don't use my notes, I forget what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your ability to remember things you’ve learned in the past, like personal life events or information you’ve studied, is still as good as it always was?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say that's still fine. I don't have any trouble remembering, you know, my past or things I learned years ago. That stuff is all still right there in my head. \n\nIt's really just the day-to-day stuff—the things I have to do right now or tomorrow—that I need my piece of paper for. But as far as old memories and things from my life go, no, they're as good as they ever were. I haven't noticed any changes there."
